====================================================================================================

River swimming in Spree, Rhine and Danube: what Switzerland can teach us
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:14:10 UTC
16:14:10 KST

In Basel, swimmers can drift home along the river in the evening. In many German rivers, however, swimming is banned. The Rhine flows here too, so why do the two countries treat it so differently?
It is 32 degrees in Basel. On the bridge people are eating ice cream, while below it swimmers keep surfacing as they drift along in the Rhine. In Basel you are allowed to swim in the river, despite international freight shipping and other leisure activities.
"Part of the river in the city centre is marked out with buoys," Tim Edler tells Euronews. Swimmers, leisure users and shipping all share the same stretch of water here. Edler is co-founder of Flussbad Berlin, an association that wants to make the Spree in Berlin safe for swimming. He was recently in Basel for the Swimmable Cities European Forum. The aim of this network is to make urban waterways accessible again and clean enough for bathing.
And in Basel that is already reality, "one of the city's central features", as Edler puts it. The Swiss city is widely regarded as the Mecca of river swimming in major cities. "For many people who live and work here, the Rhine is an important part of their everyday lives," says Conradin Cramer, president of the Basel cantonal government, speaking to SRF.
River bathing is not a gift of nature
In mid-August around 4,500 people showed how it is done. Edler also took part in the Rhine swim, an annual event in Basel. The approximately two-kilometre course runs downstream from Kleinbasel to the Johanniter Bridge. Safe bathing is made possible by clear rules and good information, explains Christoph Bosshardt, head of Basel's location marketing, speaking to SRF.
Clean water alone is not enough. "Showers, steps, entry points, rescue plans and clear rules are just as important," Bosshardt tells the Swiss media company. In Basel the infrastructure along the Rhine has been systematically expanded over many years.
After a major fire in 1986 at a Basel chemical factory warehouse, not only chemicals but also tonnes of toxic fire-fighting water flowed into the Rhine. What followed was an ecocide – and a rethink about how the river should be used in future. Basel does not have a clean, swimmable Rhine by chance; it has worked towards it over decades. Edler also says: "This is something you can achieve through persistent, focused change."
Why the Rhine is swimmable in Basel but not in Cologne or Düsseldorf
Edler, who also wants to introduce swimming in Berlin's Spree, sees the core problem in the way waterways are managed. "The most important prerequisite that exists in Basel is not water quality, riverbanks, ports or facilities; it is the willingness to allow people to use the river on their own responsibility," says Edler.
Because in Basel too people are well aware that the Rhine has strong currents and, as a fast-flowing river, entails risks. The city and the Basel section of the Swiss Life Saving Society (SLRG) provide maps showing the safe areas.
On the website (source in German) of the City of Basel you can find information on water level and temperature. The water should be at least 18 degrees Celsius, you should always swim with others, and you should not leave the marked swimming areas. So in Basel there is a clear awareness that river swimming involves dangers.
"The authorities and politicians are not there to shield people completely from these dangers, but should instead create the conditions that allow them to deal with them in an appropriate way," Edler believes. For him, concrete information and clear rules are a better option than bans.
Basel lies where Switzerland, France and Germany meet. Here the Rhine even marks the immediate border. If you were to swim a few hundred metres further from Basel, "then you swim into France, where it is banned, and across the river in Germany it is banned as well," Edler tells Euronews. It is, of course, the same river, but it is perceived differently.
Why is river bathing failing in Germany?
In Germany, the Rhine is still a long way from becoming a giant public swimming pool. Almost 500 kilometres north of Basel lies Cologne. Here, and in neighbouring Düsseldorf, bathing and swimming in the Rhine are strictly prohibited throughout the city area. Anyone who ignores this faces fines of up to 1,000 euros.
The bans have been in force in these major cities since late summer 2025. Cologne cites numerous fatal bathing accidents as the reason. "The Rhine is particularly dangerous because you cannot see the whirlpools and suction effects, which are caused among other things by ships," the city explains. Düsseldorf and other riverside cities have issued similar bans.
Edler, however, asks why swimming is so dangerous in the first place. The Rhine in particular has been dedicated to shipping, with the riverbed modified in places. To that end, so-called groynes or training walls have been built – gravel banks or stone walls jutting into the water. It is here in particular that currents develop with a suction effect towards the navigation channel.
"If people fall into the water from one of these groynes that stick out into the river, they are automatically in an area of deadly whirlpools," Edler also warns. "It is really extremely dangerous," he says, and considers a bathing ban sensible for the time being. In the longer term, however, he argues against it: "The question is, after all, these groynes did not just fall from the sky."
Shipping has priority in Germany
"First and foremost we have an administration that develops the river in inner-city areas solely according to the principles of optimising it for shipping," Edler explains. In many large German cities the Rhine is not suitable for bathing because it is designed purely for economic use.
"It is scandalous that these bans are not linked to a requirement to improve the situation," says Edler. For him, the bathing ban is not the real problem. Rather, the problem is that dangerous riverbank conditions have been created and then swimming is simply banned.
In Edler's view, any ban should have been accompanied by conditions directed at the managers of the Rhine, in this case the federal government: "[Swimming] has to be banned, but in five years' time, dear federal government, you must have redesigned the riverbank so that we can let people back in," is Edler's proposal to the federal authorities.
In fact, especially within Cologne's city limits there used to be several bathing sites. "The municipal administrative report lists for the year 1913 in Cologne a total of six public riverbank bathing facilities, five river bathing establishments and one beach pool," the city explained. The areas where swimming was permitted were fenced off and secured. Today, the authorities are again looking for locations where exceptions might be possible. These assessments will take a while.
Historically evolved, now frozen in place
The conflict between shipping, managed by the Federal Ministry of Transport, and society's desire to use rivers for bathing also exists in Berlin. Here in particular Edler is sharply critical of the bathing ban: "The Spree in Berlin is a river where you can really say it no longer serves transport at all, but recreation."
According to Edler, the Spree and the Spree Canal are managed with the wrong basic assumption, namely that they should primarily be shipping lanes. A blanket ban on bathing applies. The Spree is still a federal waterway and is also used by cargo vessels. In inner-city Berlin, however, traditional freight transport plays a relatively minor role. Today it is passenger and leisure shipping that dominate.
The Senate states, for example, that passenger shipping accounts for around 60 percent of the CO² emissions from inland shipping in Berlin. If a significant part of today's use is local and tourism-related, Edler asks whether the Berlin stretch of the Spree should still be managed with the same priorities as an important supra-regional freight corridor.
"The blanket ban in force in Berlin blocks any projects, any initiatives, any positive development of the waterway," says Edler, who sees potential for public bathing areas in the Spree. Through his association Flussbad Berlin he wants to draw attention to this and calls for monthly protest swims (source in German), including again on 20 August. With the campaign for the state parliament elections in full swing, party representatives are also present.
Edler talks about funding of up to one million euros that cannot be used because the blanket ban blocks a pilot project. "We have, for example, specific funding for a pilot bathing site in the Spree Canal, where there is no longer any shipping," says Edler. "But this investment cannot be realised because swimming in the Spree Canal is prohibited." The ban removes both the political and practical incentive to create the conditions for safe bathing in the first place.
Yet the funds are part of the Integrated Urban Development Concept (ISEK) for Berlin's Mitte district. Its aim is to upgrade the historic centre. But the administration comes up against limits in implementation, where the Senate and ultimately the federal level would also have to be involved in decision-making.
Edler is frustrated by the standstill. "If, as a society, we fail to develop the idea that water in the city is public space that belongs to people, it is almost impossible to push through projects in this direction." Yet he is sure that river swimming will sooner or later take hold in many German cities too.
It is already on the agenda in all affluent, developed regions, especially in European cities. For instance, an event organised by Swimmable Cities (source in German), which also hosted the forum in Basel, has already taken place in Rotterdam. There have also been discussions in the context of the Olympic Games in Paris, as well as in London and Amsterdam.
River swimming as a European trend
"We still have to make this shift in awareness here," says Edler. "All the other prerequisites – hygiene standards, shipping and so on – are solvable problems." Across Europe, however, public use of waterways – especially in cities – is only a matter of time. "It is no longer acceptable for the river running through the middle of these cities to be essentially a toxic sewer; instead people want to experience and use the river as public space," Edler makes clear.
In Paris this summer, people are allowed to swim in the Canal Saint-Martin on Sunday afternoons. Lifeguards are also on duty at these times. "The atmosphere on site was very relaxed and all sorts of people come together," explains Janett Koch, who is also a member of the Flussbad Berlin association.
"The water was very pleasant and smelled fine. I had plenty of space to swim," is how Koch describes her experience. You do have to swim in a bit of a zigzag. "It was a real blessing to be able to cool off on a hot day with temperatures above 30°C. Afterwards you get back on the Metro with a spring in your step," Koch adds.
Access to the canal is easy, she says. "On the Canal Saint-Martin all you need are a few ropes to demarcate the bathing zone and a pontoon with ladders." There is also a forecasting system for water quality. "From my point of view, Paris could easily allow these swimming activities to continue beyond July and August," is Koch's conclusion.

Russia Targets Ukraine-Moldova Border Checkpoint as Moscow Unleashes a Multi-Layered Attack
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:14:03 UTC
16:14:03 KST

Russian forces launched a massive overnight attack on Ukraine using ballistic and cruise missiles as well as 168 Shahed-type drones, killing at least 13 people and injuring around 40, President Volodymyr Zelensky said on Aug. 20.
The main target of the attack was the Kyiv region, according to Ukraine’s Air Force. Kyiv was hit particularly hard, with residential buildings, a high-rise, a children’s hospital, and a school damaged, Zelensky said.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
“As of now, it is known that this Russian strike took the lives of 12 people in Kyiv, there is one person who died in the region,” Zelensky said on Telegram.
He expressed condolences to the victims’ families and said emergency and rescue operations were continuing.
According to the president, Russia also struck infrastructure in Odesa and Chernihiv regions. In the Odesa region, drones targeted a checkpoint on the border with Moldova, while Russian forces struck gas infrastructure in the Chernihiv region.
Russia used ballistic, cruise missiles, and drones
The Ukrainian Air Force said Russia began the attack at around 6 p.m. on Aug. 19, launching missiles and drones against Ukraine.
The ballistic component of the attack included Onyx, Zircon, Iskander-M, and S-400 missiles launched from Russia’s Kursk, Rostov, and Bryansk regions. The Air Force did not disclose how many ballistic missiles were fired.
Kyiv Post correspondents in the capital counted at least 20 explosions during the ballistic attack.
Ukraine’s Government Removes Sense Bank Chairman Amid Anti-Corruption Probe
Russia also launched 36 Kh-101, Iskander-K, and Kh-59 cruise and aviation missiles from areas in Vologda, Kursk, and Belgorod regions, according to the Air Force.
Eight Kalibr cruise missiles were launched from the Novorossiysk area, along with two Banderol loitering munitions.
The attack also involved 168 Shahed-type strike drones, Gerbera and Parody decoy drones, and other unmanned systems. Almost half of the Shahed-type drones were reportedly jet-powered.
The Air Force said the attack was primarily directed at the Kyiv region.
Ukraine says it intercepted most cruise missiles and drones
Ukraine’s Air Force said its defenses used fighter aircraft, anti-aircraft missile units, electronic warfare, unmanned systems and mobile fire groups to repel the attack.
As of 9:30 a.m., Ukrainian forces had shot down or suppressed 31 of the 36 Kh-101, Iskander-K and Kh-59 cruise missiles, all eight Kalibr missiles, and both Banderol loitering munitions, according to preliminary Air Force data.
Air defenses also shot down or suppressed 145 Shahed, Gerbera, and other drones.
The Air Force said aerial targets were hit at 28 locations, while debris from intercepted weapons fell at two locations.
Zelensky calls for more ballistic missile defenses
Zelensky said the scale of the attack showed that Russia had been preparing for it for some time and deliberately combined different types of weapons to maximize damage to civilian infrastructure.
“While Moscow is investing in ballistics and escalation, the world does not always have the necessary reaction to such strikes,” Zelenskyy said.
He argued that Russia would have little incentive to seriously pursue peace while Ukraine lacks sufficient defenses against ballistic missiles.
“We are doing everything to ensure protection, and work is underway every day on the FREYJA program,” Zelensky said, referring to Ukraine’s efforts to develop additional air-defense capabilities.
He added that Patriot interceptor missiles have not yet been replaced and are needed on a daily basis.
“Each additional missile saves the lives of our people,” the president said.
Zelensky thanked rescuers, doctors, police officers and utility workers who have been responding to the aftermath of the attack since the first hours.

South Korean President Lee hopes Trump's reduction of joint military drills leads to peace on Korean Peninsula
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20

16:14:00 KST

Islamuddin Sajid
20 August 2026•Update: 20 August 2026
South Korean President Lee Jae Myung on Wednesday backed US President Donald Trump’s decision to scale back joint military exercises with South Korea, expressing hope the move would help build trust with North Korea and revive dialogue on the Korean Peninsula, according to media reports.
Lee said in a message on US social media platform X that he supported Trump’s efforts to establish a peace regime by reducing combined exercises and outdoor maneuver drills, describing the decision as a major step away from hostility and confrontation, the Chosun Biz news outlet reported.
His statement came after Trump instructed the Pentagon to "substantially reduce" joint military exercises with South Korea, citing their cost and his relationship with the North Korean leader.
The annual US-South Korea Ulchi Freedom Shield military exercises, which began Monday, were subsequently reduced and will end Friday, instead of its routine 11-day schedule.
Lee said a peace system in which there was no need to fight would be “the most perfect security asset,” and expressed hope that the move would lead to renewed US-North Korea talks and lasting stability.
He noted his pledge to serve as a “pace maker” on the peninsula and said he would continue supporting Trump’s efforts to promote dialogue and address the North Korean nuclear issue.
Lee said South Korea would at the same time significantly increase defense spending to strengthen its ability to take greater responsibility for defending the peninsula.
He added that Seoul was moving toward defense spending equivalent to 3.5% of gross domestic product (GDP), in line with an agreement between the two allies.
Lee also said South Korea would continue working with the international community, including the US, to help restore freedom of navigation in the Strait of Hormuz, within the limits allowed by South Korean law and policy.
Lee said he looked forward to meeting Trump again in the US.
Trump on Monday put the number of US troops in South Korea at 39,000 while criticizing Seoul for not supporting Washington’s military operation against Iran.
“We have 39,000 soldiers over there guarding you (South Korea) from Kim Jong Un, your next-door neighbor, and you're not going to help us on a very easy military operation in Iran? That's strange,” said Trump.

Taiwan court sentences association official over pre-election China trip
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:13:22 UTC
16:13:22 KST

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — The Taipei District Court on Thursday sentenced an official of an association representing people from China's Anhui Province to four years and six months in prison for organizing a group trip to China to influence voting in the 2024 presidential election.
Taiwan Anhui Association Secretary General Lee An-tai (李安泰) violated the Anti-infiltration Act and the Presidential and Vice Presidential Election and Recall Act, the court ruled. The ruling can be appealed, per Liberty Times.
At the request of Chinese officials in Anhui, Lee and Taipei Anhui Association Chair Sung Kuo-chien (宋國倩) organized a trip for 24 association members, friends, and local neighborhood leaders to Anhui in October and November 2023. During the trip, they were called on to vote for KMT presidential candidate Hou Yu-ih (侯友宜) in the January 2024 presidential election, prosecutors said.
While participants had to pay for flights between Taiwan and Anhui and their first night at a hotel, the province’s Taiwan Affairs Office spent an estimated NT$33,000 (US$1,000) per person on other expenses, including food and transportation.
During dinners, Taiwan Affairs Office representatives delivered speeches repeating known Chinese government views, including the “one China principle” and opposition to Taiwan independence, Liberty Times reported.
Although Lee knew that Chinese government departments were behind the trip, he still proceeded with it, the court said. Sung faced similar charges but was acquitted by the Taipei District Court on Thursday.

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — New Power Party Chair Wang Wan-yu (王婉諭) on Thursday cautioned against tying the pro-Taiwan cause exclusively to the DPP, rejecting political commentator Wang Hao’s (汪浩) call for her to join the DPP.
Wang Hao on Wednesday called on Taipei City Councilor Miao Po-ya (苗博雅), Wang Wan-yu, and leaders of other smaller parties to follow the example of DPP Legislator Fan Yun (范雲), who was previously a member of the Social Democratic Party, and join the DPP to consolidate Taiwan’s pro-Taiwan political forces, per Liberty Times. He said he believed the fragmentation of the pro-Taiwan camp could allow the KMT to return to power.

She stressed that safeguarding Taiwan requires contributions from all political parties, rather than relying on a single party to protect the country.
Green Party Co-convener Gan Chong-wei (甘崇緯) said supporting Taiwan should not mean abandoning oversight of the DPP. He said previous analyses of third-party voters found that supporters of the NPP, Green Party, and other smaller parties came from similar sources as those of the TPP, rather than primarily from the DPP, per FTNN.
Gan warned that reducing Taiwan’s political landscape to a DPP-versus-KMT and TPP contest could risk losing support from third-party voters.
Taiwan Statebuilding Party Chair Wang Hsing-huan (王興煥), citing a recent survey, said roughly half of respondents opposed both China and the DPP, per FTNN. He stressed that diversity is necessary for the pro-Taiwan camp to safeguard the country.
Taiwan Obasang Political Equality Party Co-convener Lin Shih-han (林詩涵) said the formation of the Taiwan Go Go alliance, comprising her party, the Taiwan Statebuilding Party, the Green Party, and the NPP, was an effort to consolidate pro-Taiwan forces, per Newtalk. She said the alliance aims to pool support and create an oversight force for the DPP within the pro-Taiwan camp.
Lin added that smaller parties can promote non-mainstream voices that may be overlooked by the DPP.

'Anti-national decision': Amit Shah slams Congress's stand to stick to first two stanzas of Vande Mataram
Edited by: Karan Manral / TIMESOFINDIA.COM / Updated: Aug 20, 2026, 13:19 IST

Amit Shah attacks Congress over decision to sing only first two stanzas of Vande Mataram
NEW DELHI: Union home minister Amit Shah on Thursday criticised the Congress over its decision to sing only the first two stanzas of the national song Vande Mataram, calling the move "anti-national."“Yesterday, the Congress Working Committee took an anti-national decision. Following its 1937 resolution, the Congress decided to sing only two stanzas of Vande Mataram. I want to remind the nation that in 1937, by dividing Vande Mataram for the appeasement of Muslims, the Congress laid the foundation for the partition of the country,” Shah told ANI in Mamallapuram, Tamil Nadu.
He further stated that the Narendra Modi government, on the other hand, had corrected the Congress' “mistake” and given Vande Mataram a legal framework.“Congress's decision confirms its policy of appeasement. They have not only insulted this creation of Bankim Babu but also lakhs of people who laid down their lives for the freedom of the country. Today, the Congress party is implementing its 1937 resolution but rejecting a law made by Parliament. I am surprised that the Congress party, under the leadership of Rahul Gandhi, is once again taking forward appeasement politics,” Shah added, referring to poet Bankim Chandra Chatterjee, who wrote Vande Mataram.
“The country has once made the mistake of letting Vande Mataram be divided into two parts, and we have faced its consequences. Now, the country should come together against this decision of the Congress party and say that their politics of appeasement will not work anymore. BJP condemns this decision of the Congress party,” the home minister remarked.In response, the Congress alleged that the BJP was trying to divert attention from a range of issues.""They want to divert attention from the genuine issues of paper leaks, theft of donations and Chinese incursion in Arunachal Pradesh. We have been reciting Vande Mataram since 1937, more than 90 years. What is new for us? BJP was not a part of the freedom struggle. Vande Mataram was a part of the freedom struggle; it is emotional for us, but political for them," Congress national general secretary (organisation) KC Venugopal said.
Shah's attack on the principal opposition party comes days after Vande Mataram was played for the first time at the Independence Day celebrations. All six stanzas of the national song were played before and after Prime Minister Narendra Modi's address to the nation from the iconic Red Fort in Delhi.
At a meeting of the Congress Working Committee (CWC), the party's highest decision-making body, on Wednesday, the Congress resolved to continue singing only the first two stanzas, in line with the 1937 resolution and the position adopted by the Constituent Assembly.Congress president Mallikarjun Kharge elaborated on the issue by reading out the 1937 CWC resolution, arguing that Mahatma Gandhi, Jawaharlal Nehru, Sardar Patel and Subhas Chandra Bose had approved singing only the first two stanzas of Vande Mataram on the advice of Rabindranath Tagore.

Lai says Taiwan to advance net-zero tech globally

07:12:42 UTC
16:12:42 KST

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — Taiwan will continue developing key net-zero technologies and help promising research become commercial products for international markets, President Lai Ching-te (賴清德) said Wednesday, per the Presidential Office.
Speaking at the awards ceremony for the 2026 Net Zero Tech International Contest, Lai praised young researchers for using technology to address climate challenges. He said Taiwan has strong research capabilities and well-developed industrial supply chains.
The government will continue supporting net-zero tech development and strengthen cooperation among government agencies, companies, universities, and research institutions, Lai said. It will also provide testing sites, industry partnerships, and startup support to help turn research into practical products.
Lai said much of the world's emissions reductions so far have come from wider use of existing technologies. However, reaching net-zero emissions by 2050 and meeting rising electricity demand from artificial intelligence will require new materials, energy systems, production methods, and more efficient computing.
Taiwan's semiconductor, information technology, and precision manufacturing industries give the country an advantage in developing these technologies, Lai said. He added that Taiwan can combine research and manufacturing strengths to create net-zero solutions that compete in international markets.
Lai also cited the national emissions reduction plan. The government aims to cut greenhouse gas emissions by 36% to 40% from 2005 levels by 2035.
The contest has grown significantly since it began in 2023 with 146 teams from 10 countries. This year, 267 teams from 20 countries took part, presenting research aimed at addressing climate and energy challenges.
Lai encouraged participants to continue developing their research and turn their ideas into practical applications. He said Taiwan is willing to support international research, cooperation, and entrepreneurship as countries work toward net-zero emissions.

Over 200,000 people newly displaced in Sudan’s Kordofan as fighting rages
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:11:37 UTC
16:11:37 KST

Over 200,000 people newly displaced in Sudan’s Kordofan as fighting rages
UN warns of surging displacement in Kordofan as power and water infrastructure come under attack in El Obeid.
More than 200,000 people have been newly displaced across Sudan’s Kordofan region due to intensified fighting since late last year, the United Nations has said, as continued drone attacks target critical water and electricity infrastructure in the region’s biggest city, El Obeid.
The warning from the UN’s International Organization for Migration (IOM) came on Wednesday as seasonal flooding in nearby North Darfur state also compounded the displacement crisis.
Recommended Stories
list of 3 items
- list 1 of 3Sudan’s war threatens an ‘entire generation’s’ future, UN warns
- list 2 of 3How a Sudanese refugee became a head teacher in a Chadian camp
- list 3 of 3Sudan’s al-Burhan pledges immunity for dialogue, rules out Hemedti return
“The warning signs are impossible to ignore,” said IOM Director General Amy Pope. “Across Sudan, families are fleeing violence, losing access to water, electricity and healthcare, and now facing the added burden of destructive floods.”
According to the IOM, displacement in the Sheikan district, which includes the city of El Obeid, has surged 25 percent over the past eight months.
In El Obeid, repeated fighting and drone attacks on the city’s main power transformer have knocked out electricity needed to pump water, run hospitals and keep phone lines working, the organisation said.
In Blue Nile state, some 78,000 people have also fled their homes, the IOM said, while in West Darfur, about 18,000 others were displaced in the first half of August.
Meanwhile in North Darfur, heavy rains in the Tawila area have damaged nearly 1,350 homes, displacing more than 1,000 families, the UN organisation added.
“Sudan is one of the world’s largest displacement crises,” Pope said. “The combined impact of conflict, infrastructure damage and flooding is stretching thousands of families beyond their limits.”
Since the war began in April 2023, an estimated 59,000 people have been killed, while 8.6 million people have been internally displaced. More than 4.2 million people have also fled to neighbouring countries.
Some 33.7 million people require humanitarian assistance, according to the UN.
Reporting from one of the main roads leading to El Obeid, Al Jazeera’s Almigdad Alruhaid described a route where drones belonging to the Rapid Support Forces (RSF) paramilitary have targeted army vehicles, oil tankers and cargo trucks.
The Sudanese army-held city has endured one of the longest sieges of the war, but the RSF “still maintains position south and west to the city and still continues attacking nearby villages”, Alruhaid said, adding that this was further worsening the humanitarian situation in the region.
El Obeid had a population of 750,000 people before the war, but authorities now estimate the population has reached 3.4 million, according to the Sudan Tribune.
Save the Children, a humanitarian group, said that nearly 39,000 children have arrived here in the first six months of this year.

US to deport CIA informant who infiltrated al-Qaeda: Report
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:09:15 UTC
16:09:15 KST

Berk Kutay Gokmen
20 August 2026•Update: 20 August 2026
The administration of President Donald Trump is seeking to deport Blerim Skoro, a Kosovo-born father of three who says he worked undercover for US intelligence and infiltrated al-Qaeda after 9/11, CBS News reported Wednesday.
Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) arrested the 55-year-old outside an immigration office in New Jersey on Aug. 3, where he had gone to renew his paperwork.
His lawyers have asked a federal court to halt the deportation, arguing Skoro was promised residency after years of work as a “CIA agent.”
Now a Staten Island cab driver with an American wife and children, Skoro received protection from deportation in 2022 because of the danger he could face in Kosovo.
His lawyers say his cooperation helped authorities arrest members of al-Qaeda, al-Shabaab and Hezbollah.
“I never thought they were going to betray me like this,” he told CBS News from the Elizabeth Detention Center. “Please, if I die, I should die in this country so my kids can bury me.”
According to FBI records, Skoro began cooperating with US authorities after being imprisoned for heroin trafficking in 2000.
From 2002 to 2006, while serving his sentence in Pennsylvania, Skoro provided information on suspected militants.
FBI documents identified him as an informant in an investigation involving Osama bin Laden and described some of his information as “actionable” and “reliable.”
After being deported to Kosovo in 2007, Skoro said the CIA instructed him to infiltrate al-Qaeda.
He claimed he operated undercover from 2007 to 2009, traveling through several countries and visiting an ISIS/Daesh camp in Syria.
His attorney, Joshua Dratel, said Skoro has since lived a “completely law-abiding, productive life with his family” after what he called a “harrowing experience” carried out for the United States.
Skoro said his cover was eventually exposed and that he survived an assassination attempt before returning to the US in 2014. His immigration case has continued for years.
In 2022, a federal judge barred his removal to Kosovo under the Convention Against Torture, citing the risk of torture or murder, though the ruling did not grant him permanent residency.
His lawyers argued ICE violated that protection by attempting to deport him without first obtaining judicial permission.

Live-streamed Philippine school shooting raises alarm over ‘memetic violence’
Analysts say the Zamboanga attack appears to fit a pattern of violence shaped by online spectacle, imitation and the pursuit of notoriety
The attack took place on Tuesday morning at the junior high school campus of the privately run Ateneo de Zamboanga University in Zamboanga City, where police said a student brought weapons onto campus, opened fire inside a classroom and killed a fellow student. Investigators are still examining the sequence of events and the student’s motive.
Zamboanga Mayor Khymer Adan Olaso said in a radio interview on DZBB that the student had live-streamed the attack through a body-worn camera. Olaso also said that the gunman shot at a teacher and a Grade 10 student before taking his own life.
The shooter’s father, a customs police officer, was relieved of his duties after the attack as investigators looked into reports that the firearms used belonged to him.

====================================================================================================

Once again, Israeli air strikes are massacring people in Gaza
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:07:22 UTC
16:07:22 KST

Breadcrumb
What ceasefire? Once again, Israeli air strikes are massacring people in Gaza
Israel attacked the Gaza Strip after days of contacts and meetings aimed at advancing the fragile ceasefire to its next stage, raising renewed fears that the truce could collapse and the war could return on a wider scale.
At least 15 Palestinians were killed and dozens injured by two Israeli airstrikes on Gaza City within less than 24 hours.
The first, on Tuesday evening, hit a beachside café in the port area. The second, on Wednesday afternoon, hit a municipal police headquarters in the city centre, killing at least nine people and injuring around 15 others, according to Palestinian medical sources.
The strikes came as US, Egyptian and Qatari mediators intensified efforts to advance negotiations over the second phase of the ceasefire agreement.
The talks remain stalled over several fundamental issues, including an Israeli withdrawal from Gaza, Hamas's weapons, and the future administration and security of the territory.
Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu has insisted that any future arrangement must guarantee Israel's security and has vowed that Israel would not leave Gaza.
Hamas, meanwhile, says the question of its disarmament cannot be separated from an end to the genocidal war, an Israeli withdrawal and guarantees that the agreement will be implemented.
Nine killed at police headquarters
At least nine Palestinians, including a woman, were killed on Wednesday afternoon when an Israeli airstrike struck the Hamas-run municipal police headquarters in central Gaza City.
The Gaza police force said Israeli aircraft targeted the headquarters, causing deaths and injuries.
Medical sources said some victims arrived at hospitals in pieces, while around 15 wounded people were being treated, several of them in critical condition.
Palestinian sources identified six of those killed as Brigadier General Abu Akram Jundiya, Colonel Abu Abdullah al-Hajin, Colonel Abu Khalil Tamraz, Major Abu Bilal Abu Sam'an, Colonel Abu Omar Aliyan and Colonel Faten al-Sahhar, who headed the women's police force in Gaza City. The identities of the remaining victims were still being verified.
Witnesses said the strike caused a powerful explosion and scattered shrapnel across the surrounding area.
The headquarters is located near a municipal park and tents sheltering displaced Palestinians, according to local sources.
Residents described scenes of panic following the blast, saying the area had been busy with civilian activity when the attack took place.
Gaza's Ministry of Interior and National Security accused Israel of carrying out a "brutal massacre" against members of the civilian police force and appealed to mediators and the international community to intervene.
"The occupation is working to dismantle the police force in order to spread chaos within Palestinian society," the ministry said.
Hamas described the strike as "a new Zionist war crime", saying it demonstrated the Netanyahu government's refusal to implement the ceasefire agreement and move towards its next phase.
The movement also rejected Israeli claims that attacks on police personnel were aimed at resistance leaders, calling such assertions a justification for targeting civilians and members of the police.
Less than a day earlier, another Israeli strike had killed six Palestinians at a café in Gaza City's port area.
Six people, including a child, were killed and 14 wounded when an Israeli airstrike hit the "Helwa wal Murra" café on Tuesday evening, according to Gaza's Ministry of Health and medical sources.
Ismail al-Thawabta, director general of Gaza's Government Media Office, said the victims were taken to al-Shifa Medical Complex.
Witnesses said the café was frequented by civilians and that the force of the explosion threw several people into the sea, prompting rescue teams to search the water.
The six killed were identified as Ezz El-Din Salem Deeb, Jihad Muhammad Hamdi Al-Masdi, Muhammad Hamdi Ahmad Al-Masri, Essam Muhammad Musa Murtaja, Muhammad Fathi Hussein Nasser and Samed Samir Harb Abu Habel.
The Israeli army claimed it had targeted "leaders in an elite Hamas unit during a meeting", including a company commander and three faction leaders.
It alleged that those targeted were involved in planning attacks against Israeli forces in Gaza and said one had participated in the 7 October 2023 attack.
A Hamas source, who preferred not to be named, told The New Arab that the members targeted at the café were unarmed and had been meeting as part of preparations to consolidate the movement's weapons and implement US President Donald Trump's plan.
The claim adds further sensitivity to the attack because Hamas's weapons remain one of the most contentious issues in negotiations over the next phase of the ceasefire.
Fears of more war
The recent increase in Israeli violence also extended beyond airstrikes. Local sources said an Israeli special forces unit carried out an operation in southern Gaza early Wednesday, arresting a senior Hamas police officer.
Witnesses in the al-Mawasi area of Khan Younis said a force of around 15 to 20 soldiers, accompanied by dogs, entered from the al-Shouka area and reached the tent of Colonel Sabri Abdelaal before arresting him along with Nash'at Za'arab, who works for a security company.
The force subsequently withdrew towards the Israeli-held "yellow line", according to the sources.
The operation, coinciding with the strike on the municipal police headquarters, has raised questions about Israel's approach to Gaza's security and police structures at a time when negotiators are discussing who will administer and police the territory during the next phase.
The recent violence occurred during a sensitive political time. US envoy Jared Kushner met Hamas officials in Cairo and then traveled to Israel for talks with Netanyahu, as Washington, Egypt, and Qatar urged parties to implement the second phase of the ceasefire.
Major disagreements remain: Israel demands Hamas's disarmament and security guarantees, while Hamas wants an end to the war and Israeli withdrawal before discussing weapons.
Netanyahu emphasises Israel's security, which Hamas sees as enabling future military action. Reports also say Netanyahu plans to meet opposition leader Yair Lapid to brief him on security issues amid mounting political and security pressures.
In Gaza, residents and analysts say the renewed attacks demonstrate how distant the language of ceasefire negotiations remains from reality on the ground.
Gaza-based political analyst Taysir Abdullah told TNA that Israel's use of high-explosive weapons in densely populated areas makes civilian casualties almost inevitable.
"What does the occupation expect when it launches a missile weighing a ton of explosives into the midst of displaced people's tents, whatever the justification or reason?" he said. "Yesterday, six were killed in Gaza's port, and today nine were killed in the municipal park. Does he think he's performing delicate surgery with a laser endoscope?"
"These are piles of tightly packed flesh. If you hit a small stone, you'd hit two or three people, so what about a missile?" he added.
Abdullah questioned whether the attacks could genuinely be described as targeted operations against wanted individuals, arguing that civilians continue to bear the consequences.
"These are massacres for which children, women, and the elderly pay the price daily," he said, warning that continued attacks during negotiations could undermine efforts to reach a durable agreement.
He also criticised Palestinian factions meeting in Cairo for what he described as passivity, urging them to take action rather than limiting themselves to statements of condemnation.
Between negotiations and bombardment
For Gaza's residents, the ceasefire remains an abstraction when explosions can return without warning.
"We don't understand how we can talk about a second phase, an agreement, and mediators, while at the same time we hear explosions and see people falling before our eyes," Gaza City resident Ibrahim Abu Shaaban told TNA.
"If negotiations are underway, who will stop the bombing? And who can guarantee that the agreement will hold if Israel can bomb whenever it wants?" he asked.
Another resident, Mohammed Odwan, said fear had returned to Gaza's streets.
"Every day we hear news about negotiations, then the bombing comes and kills people," he said. "For us, it makes no difference whether they say the war is over or it continues, as long as death reaches our tents, cafés and streets."

Latvia may be forced to turn to nuclear energy amid growing electricity demand
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:07:09 UTC
16:07:09 KST

Latvia will have to consider the use of nuclear energy in the future as energy consumption by the economy and data centres increases, while the Baltic states continue to face an electricity deficit, Arvils Ašeradens, New Unity’s candidate for prime minister and a member of the Saeima, said in an interview on LETA’s podcast “Politiķi! Kas tas vispār bija?!”.
According to Ašeradens, the Baltic states currently face an electricity shortfall of approximately 20% to 30%, meaning that some of the energy they need has to be imported. This, in turn, contributes to higher electricity prices and hampers industrial development.
The politician said that one possible solution in the future could be modular, or small-scale, nuclear power plants. In his view, such projects would be better suited to the Baltic states than the construction of large-scale nuclear power plants.
Ašeradens acknowledged that implementing such solutions is currently still too expensive, but predicted that more viable technological solutions could become available over time. He also referred to assessments by his Estonian counterparts regarding the prospects for developing such projects.
At the same time, Ašeradens said that the most challenging issue in implementing nuclear energy projects could be securing public acceptance for potential locations.
According to the politician, the issue of nuclear energy will become increasingly relevant as the economy becomes more dependent on electricity, particularly due to the operation of data centres, where energy demand is growing very rapidly.
Ašeradens also noted that a large proportion of data centres serving the Baltic region are located in Helsinki because Finland has access to nuclear energy.
LETA’s podcast series “Politiķi! Kas tas vispār bija?!” interviews prime ministerial candidates from political parties that have a realistic chance of winning seats in the next Saeima.

SYDNEY, Aug. 20 (Xinhua) -- Four people are receiving medical treatment after a fire broke out in the intensive care unit at a major hospital in Western Australia (WA) on Thursday.
The fire was reported in a self-contained room at the public Fiona Stanley Hospital in the southern suburbs of Perth around 10 a.m. local time on Thursday.
The fire was extinguished and did not spread beyond the room, but a spokesperson for WA's South Metropolitan Health Service told the Australian Broadcasting Corporation that three staff members and a patient were undergoing medical assessment.
They said that some services have been temporarily closed due to water damage and smoke impact and the hospital has imposed cautionary measures, including patient relocation and short-term ambulance diversions.
"Non-urgent visitors are urged to postpone their visit to the hospital, while our staff work to return services to normal," the spokesperson said.
The Fiona Stanley Hospital has 783 beds for patients. ■

====================================================================================================

China sentences former Evergrande boss to life in prison
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
15:06:52 CST
16:06:52 KST

Xu Jiayin, former boss of Evergrande Group, stands trial at the Shenzhen Intermediate People's Court in Shenzhen, south China's Guangdong Province, Aug. 20, 2026. A Chinese court on Thursday sentenced Xu Jiayin, former boss of Evergrande Group, to life in prison after convicting him of multiple crimes. (Shenzhen Intermediate People's Court/Handout via Xinhua)
SHENZHEN, Aug. 20 (Xinhua) -- A Chinese court on Thursday sentenced Xu Jiayin, former boss of Evergrande Group, to life in prison after convicting him of multiple crimes.
The Shenzhen Intermediate People's Court in Guangdong Province found Evergrande Group and Xu guilty of illegally absorbing public deposits, fundraising fraud, illegally issuing loans, fraudulent issuance of securities, unlawful disclosure of important information, and corporate bribery.
The court also convicted Xu of illegally using funds and embezzlement, and Evergrande Real Estate Group of fraudulent issuance of securities.
According to the court, Xu was the actual controller of Evergrande Group, with overall responsibility for its operations. He managed and controlled Evergrande Real Estate Group and several other companies, with investments spanning real estate, property management, finance and other sectors.
The criminal acts of Xu and the two companies seriously disrupted the socialist market economy, infringed upon public and private property rights, and undermined the integrity of public officials in the performance of their duties, the court said, adding that given the exceptionally large sums involved, the particularly egregious circumstances, the extraordinarily heavy economic losses caused, and the extremely serious harm to society, they should be severely punished in accordance with the law.
Xu was deprived of his political rights for life, and all his personal assets were ordered confiscated. The court also ordered the continued recovery of his illegal gains and restitution for any remaining shortfall.
Evergrande Group was fined 8.82 billion yuan (1.3 billion U.S. dollars), and Evergrande Real Estate Group was fined 7 billion yuan.
According to the court findings, between 2016 and 2021, Evergrande Group, Evergrande Real Estate Group and Xu, in violation of laws and regulations, inflated assets and concealed liabilities through sustained, large-scale financial fraud and other means.
Evergrande Group and Xu gained control over financial institutions through bribery and other means, illegally obtaining credit and insurance funds for use by Evergrande Group. Xu also took advantage of his position as chairman of Evergrande Real Estate Group to organize financial fraud and misappropriated company property under the guise of dividends.
In addition, the court and a local district court also pronounced judgments against those tied to the case of Evergrande Group for illegally absorbing public deposits, fundraising fraud, and illegal use of funds.
The courts sentenced 56 individuals to imprisonment ranging from 22 months to 18 years. ■

A Chinese court on Thursday sentenced Xu Jiayin, former boss of Evergrande Group, to life in prison after convicting him of multiple crimes. (Shenzhen Intermediate People's Court/Handout via Xinhua)

Malaysia CPO prices seen firm as supply surplus narrows
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
15:06:43 CST
16:06:43 KST

KUALA LUMPUR, Aug. 20 (Xinhua) -- Malaysia's crude palm oil (CPO) prices are expected to remain elevated through the rest of 2026 and into early 2027, as stronger biodiesel demand and the risk of an El Niño-driven supply disruption tighten the global vegetable oils market, research houses said.
BMI Country Risk and Industry Research has raised its 2026 average price forecast for front-month Bursa Malaysia-listed CPO futures to 4,453 ringgit (1,103 U.S. dollars) per ton, from 4,300 ringgit previously, citing a narrowing global production surplus as consumption growth outpaces supply.
"We expect global palm oil production to reach 81.4 million tons in the 2026/27 season, a marginal decline of just 20,000 tons from 2025/26," BMI said in a note on Wednesday.
Global consumption is forecast to rise 2.7 percent year on year to 79.9 million tons, reducing the production surplus from 3.6 million tons in 2025/26.
BMI expects prices to remain strong into the first quarter of 2027 before gradually easing as El Niño risks dissipate.
It forecasts CPO prices to average 4,543 ringgit per ton in 2027, slightly higher than its 2026 forecast, supported by the carry-over of strong prices in the second quarter and new structural demand from expanding biodiesel mandates.
The Malaysian Palm Oil Council (MPOC) also expects CPO prices to remain firm above 4,600 ringgit per ton in September, supported by tightening supply fundamentals and continued geopolitical disruptions to global trade flows.
MPOC said forward contracts for 2027 CPO futures on Bursa Malaysia Derivatives were trading above 5,000 ringgit per ton as of mid-August, reflecting market concerns over the potential impact of El Niño.
Indonesia's palm oil demand could also strengthen as the three-month transition period to clear remaining B40 biodiesel stocks ends in September, it said.
"However, downside risks remain," MPOC said, citing an easing of Black Sea logistical bottlenecks, the arrival of new-crop sunflower oil supplies in export markets, and lower energy prices if geopolitical tensions improve. These factors could trigger a correction in vegetable oil prices, it added.
Kenanga Research similarly expects edible oil prices, including palm oil, to remain elevated through the second half of 2026 and into 2027, supported by higher biodiesel demand and the risk of a severe El Niño.
The research house maintained its 2026 CPO price forecast at 4,400 ringgit per ton and its 2027 forecast at 4,450 ringgit per ton. ■

RIO DE JANEIRO, Aug. 19 (Xinhua) -- Giorgian de Arrascaeta and Samuel Lino scored in each half as Flamengo reached the Copa Libertadores quarterfinals with a 2-1 home win over Cruzeiro on Wednesday.
De Arrascaeta opened the scoring with a 35th-minute strike, but the visitors equalized through Lucas Romero's long-range effort early in the second half.

Switzerland approves 20th sanctions package against Russia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:06:37 UTC
16:06:37 KST

Switzerland approves 20th sanctions package against Russia
In response to Russia's ongoing war against Ukraine, the Swiss government has approved the 20th package of sanctions against Russia, thereby aligning itself with the European Union.
Currently, around 2,790 individuals, companies and organisations in Switzerland are subject to asset freezes in connection with Russia’s war against Ukraine.
In the energy sector, the Swiss government on Wednesday issued new service bans concerning liquefied natural gas (LNG) tankers, icebreakers and LNG terminals based in Russia. In addition, the sale of tankers to Russia is now prohibited. Similarly, agreements for the sale of tankers to third countries must now include a contractual clause prohibiting their transfer to Russia.
Switzerland is also, for the first time – just like the EU – activating the so-called anti-circumvention tool. This is designed to effectively prevent the circumvention of existing sanctions via third countries. In practical terms, the measure means that the export of certain sensitive goods to Kyrgyzstan is now prohibited.
More
Russia’s killer drones still boast Swiss components. How come?
In addition, the federal government has decided to extend the existing export ban to cover goods intended to strengthen Russia militarily and technologically, as well as goods intended to bolster Russian industry. Finally, further restrictions are being introduced on the import of economically significant goods that generate substantial revenue for Russia.
Measures in the financial sector
In the financial sector, the government has now banned the use of Russian platforms for the transfer and exchange of cryptocurrencies. The measure is intended to prevent Russia from using alternative means of payment to circumvent sanctions. In the same vein, support for the development of certain Russian cryptocurrencies, such as the digital Ruble, is prohibited.
The government has also adopted measures to protect Swiss companies, in particular to safeguard their intellectual property rights and to protect them from abusive Russian court rulings.

Cambodia establishes green finance working group to drive sustainable growth
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
15:06:31 CST
16:06:31 KST

PHNOM PENH, Aug. 20 (Xinhua) -- Cambodia has established a Green Finance Working Group (GFWG) to mobilize capital and resources for eco-friendly development.
According to a decision signed by Prime Minister Hun Manet and publicized on Thursday, the GFWG will be led by a representative from the National Bank of Cambodia, with a deputy head from the Ministry of Environment. Members will also include officials from various other ministries and institutions.
"The GFWG has roles and responsibilities to lead and coordinate the preparation and implementation of policies, strategies, and priority work directions for the development and mobilization of green finance in Cambodia," the prime minister said.
He added that the group will develop necessary support mechanisms and related frameworks to build a robust ecosystem for green finance. Furthermore, the GFWG is tasked with promoting research, training, and knowledge dissemination to keep pace with new development trends.
To foster green finance in the Southeast Asian country, the group will coordinate and strengthen cooperation with relevant ministries, local institutions, and development partners.
The GFWG "will be obliged to promote and support the development of green financial projects and instruments to support sustainable development," Hun Manet said. ■

Iran plans to bar hostile vessels from transiting Strait of Hormuz
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
15:06:30 CST
16:06:30 KST

TEHRAN, Aug. 20 (Xinhua) -- A senior Iranian lawmaker said Wednesday that a draft plan under review by Iran's parliament to safeguard security in the Strait of Hormuz would bar hostile and espionage vessels from transiting the strategic waterway.
In an interview with state-run IRIB TV, Ebrahim Rezaei, a member of the parliament's national security and foreign policy committee, elaborated on the plan, whose general outlines have been approved by the committee.
The plan stipulates that vessels deemed hostile or involved in espionage activities that could threaten Iran's national security would not be allowed to pass through the strait, Rezaei said.
He added that Israeli military or civilian vessels would also be prohibited from transiting the waterway, with enforcement to be supervised by Iran's General Staff of the Armed Forces.
Rezaei said the plan included provisions for charging passage fees in the Iranian rial or other currencies, as well as penalties for vessels violating the regulations.
"If someone in another part of the world harms Iran's interests or our citizens, we will deal with that government in the Strait of Hormuz," he said, adding that the plan allows for measures including cargo confiscation and higher tariffs against governments that take hostile positions toward Iran.
Rezaei said the Strait of Hormuz had become a national security concern for Iran following U.S. and Israeli strikes against Iran, and that Tehran must maintain control of the waterway to prevent military threats.
The parliamentary committee approved the general outlines of the plan earlier this month, Iran's official news agency IRNA reported. ■

RIO DE JANEIRO, Aug. 19 (Xinhua) -- Tomas Cuello scored in the 93rd minute as Atletico Mineiro progressed to the Copa Sudamericana quarterfinals on Wednesday with a 2-2 home draw against Bragantino.

US officials coordinated deportation flights with Iran, emails reveal
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:06:28 UTC
16:06:28 KST

The deportation of Iranians to a country whose government has a pattern of violent persecution against women, religious minorities and political dissidents has drawn significant criticism from human rights activists.
US immigration officials coordinated with Iranian authorities to deport Iranian nationals in 2025, newly released emails show, despite rising tensions between Washington and Tehran.
Hundreds of emails obtained by the National Iranian American Council and released on Tuesday detail how officials arranged three deportation flights that returned more than 100 Iranians to Iran between September 2025 and January 2026.
The emails suggest Iranian officials had some influence over which Iranian nationals were deported, with US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) officials appearing to accept some last-minute changes to deportation lists requested by Tehran.
“Per request from the Iran Embassy, I added a few cases," one unnamed ICE official wrote in late August, a month before the first deportation flight in September.
Just over a week later, someone with the same job title moved to make another, unspecified change to the deportation list after they said they met with the “Director at the Iranian Embassy.”
“Iran has requested that I amend the previous manifest and expedite the removal process,” the official wrote.
Iranian officials selected some deportees
Iranian officials said in September 2025 that up to 400 Iranians could be returned under an agreement with the Trump administration. Most had entered the US illegally through Mexico, while others faced different immigration issues, according to Tehran.
The emails do not make clear how often US immigration officials were in direct contact with the Iranian government. Some communications were relayed through Qatari officials, who helped arrange deportation flights via Doha, while others referred to regular meetings with the “Iranian delegation,” the “Director at the Iranian Embassy”, and other unnamed Iranian officials.
It is not clear whether the people Iran put on the list had volunteered to be repatriated or if they were forced. Previous reporting from The New York Times revealed that asylum-seekers were among those deported to Iran, while others said they were deported against their will.
The emails suggest that the push to carry out the deportations came from senior levels of the Trump administration, even during the 12-day conflict involving the US, Israel and Iran in June 2025.
Lawsuit alleges ICE shared asylum information with Iran
The emails show that, on several occasions, ICE officials arranged meetings for unspecified reasons between Iranian officials and people detained in the US. The accounts echo testimony from Iranian asylum-seekers in an unrelated lawsuit filed in July by an unaffiliated Iranian interest group.
Eleven Iranians in immigration detention said they were forced to meet with Iranian government officials while in ICE custody. According to sworn declarations filed in a Washington DC court in July, the Iranian officials knew intimate details about their asylum claims.
The lawsuit alleges that US immigration agencies have been illegally sharing confidential information about Iranian asylum-seekers with the Iranian government.
The US Department of Homeland Security (DHS) strongly rejected the allegations in the lawsuit.
“These allegations that ICE shared asylum application records with the Iranian government are FALSE,” DHS said in a statement in July.
US authorities are allowed to work with foreign governments to coordinate deportations. However, federal regulations prohibit the disclosure of information that could reveal that a person facing deportation had applied for asylum.

Singapore's domestic wholesale sales rebound in Q2
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
15:06:21 CST
16:06:21 KST

SINGAPORE, Aug. 20 (Xinhua) -- Singapore's domestic wholesale sales rose 20.3 percent year-on-year in the second quarter, reversing a 1.4 percent decline in the first quarter, the Department of Statistics said on Thursday.
On a seasonally adjusted quarter-on-quarter basis, domestic wholesale sales increased 13.5 percent in the second quarter, with total sales estimated at 129 billion Singapore dollars (101.4 billion U.S. dollars).
Most industries recorded year-on-year growth. Sales in the general wholesale trade industry rose 67 percent, driven by higher sales volumes and commodity prices. Sales in the ship chandlers and bunkering industry increased 56.5 percent, mainly due to higher bunker fuel prices.
In contrast, sales in the metals, timber and construction materials industry fell 23.6 percent, mainly due to lower sales volumes of metals.
Singapore's foreign wholesale sales rose 28.6 percent year-on-year in the second quarter, accelerating from a 6.3 percent increase in the previous quarter. On a seasonally adjusted quarter-on-quarter basis, foreign wholesale sales increased 16.7 percent.
Wholesale trade is Singapore's largest services sector. ■

Liberals accuse government of backdown on aged care program
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:06:18 UTC
16:06:18 KST

Government extends home support for older Australians amid pressure
In short:
The federal government has extended the Commonwealth Home Support Program for another two years.
The opposition has labelled the move an "embarrassing, overdue backdown from Labor in the face of overwhelming evidence".
What's next?
The aged care minister said the intention was to keep the program separate from Support at Home on an ongoing basis.
The Commonwealth program helping older people with shopping, transport and meals on wheels will be extended until 2029, but the government has flagged its intention to make the program permanent beyond that.
The Commonwealth Home Support Program (CHSP) provides low-level support to 830,000 seniors to help them live at home.
As part of major changes to the aged care sector, the program was due to be abolished in mid-2027 and rolled into the new Support at Home program. The new program is designed to provide a coordinated budget for more complex care, but critics have argued that the changes would make service delivery more expensive and less efficient.
Providers, including local councils, had also withdrawn services because of the uncertainty surrounding the program's future.
Aged Care Minister Sam Rae said the government was now also beginning consultation with a view to permanently keeping CHSP separate from Support at Home.
"We believe the CHSP should remain a standalone program," he said.
Mr Rae said it was important for the government to be looking at how it could use early intervention services like CHSP to take pressure off the rest of the ever-growing aged care system, which faces a shortage of beds and long waitlists.
He hoped the operators that had stopped providing services would return now they had certainty.
Health Minister Mark Butler said the CHSP program would require reform, though, because there was some overlap between it and the Support at Home program.
"There are differences in co-contribution arrangements that will have to be sorted," he said.
Under CHSP, participants are expected to contribute varying amounts to the cost of their care, if they can afford to.
"Simple services like house cleaning and meals might cost a few dollars. More complex services like some home modifications will cost much more," according to the department's website.
Under Support at Home, depending on someone's pensioner status and the type of care they require, they contribute between 5 and 80 per cent of costs.
'Overwhelming' evidence for decision
A Senate inquiry earlier this year recommended CHSP be kept as a separate block-funded program, and Shadow Aged Care Minister Anne Ruston said the decision was an "embarrassing" backdown given the mounting evidence.
"This is an embarrassing, overdue backdown from Labor in the face of overwhelming evidence that abolishing community support would have been reckless for the 835,000 older Australians who rely on this program,"
"Labor has left this decision until the last minute, just as they have done repeatedly throughout their chaotic aged care reforms."
The announcement was welcomed by provider body Ageing Australia, which had been pushing for the program to remain separate, given Support at Home was "not yet delivering for older people".
Council on the Ageing chief executive Patricia Sparrow agreed it was important the program was extended.
"The task now is making sure the Commonwealth Home Support Program, Support at Home and the broader aged care system work together so older Australians don't fall through the cracks, get caught between program boundaries or face unnecessary barriers as their needs change," she said.
"It's good that this gives people, including providers like local councils, certainty about the future of the program."
Greens leader Larissa Waters also labelled the decision a "massive win".
"It is outrageous that Labor thought that older Australians should have their lives uprooted and future thrown into uncertainty," she said.
The government did not reveal the cost of extending the program but said it was not a savings measure.

Lambie sparks veterans debate as Labor strikes deals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:05:55 UTC
16:05:55 KST

analysis
Lambie makes plea for veterans, Labor and Coalition strike deals
Welcome back to your weekly federal politics update, where Courtney Gould gets you up to speed on the happenings from Parliament House.
An impassioned Jacqui Lambie struggled to get the words out as she shared details about the medical battle behind her five-month absence from Parliament House.
Six months ago, her vagina started to prolapse and three months later, she told the Senate, it took her "bladder with it" and required surgery. Lambie said it was directly linked to her back injuries from her time serving in the army.
After hearing the story of female veteran Kate, who waited three years before she could find a gynaecologist "happy with the low prices [the Department of Veterans' Affairs] pay", Lambie took matters into her own hands.
Lambie says it's part of a broader issue with how the department treats female veterans and their health.
"I would like to ask the prime minister, minister [Richard] Marles, and minister [Matt] Keogh what they would do if their penises were hanging by a thread, and what they would do if treatment for that injury directly connected to their military service was denied by the Department of Veterans' Affairs," she said.
Loading...
Lambie used the speech to draw focus on the government's contentious proposed $5000 cap on veterans' allied health spending, which covers everything from chiropractors to physiotherapy.
The cap was announced in the budget and is aimed at tackling dodgy providers. It doesn't come into effect until next year and the government argues that veterans will still be able to apply for more funding where it is clinically indicated.
But veterans are sceptical about the department's ability to administer the cap. Lambie's intervention came as the Coalition used Question Time to exploit the rift between the veterans' affairs minister and former defence personnel on the matter.
Keogh had initially knocked back a request to meet with The Coalition of Veterans' Voice, which is campaigning against the cap. After pressure was applied, he conceded he'd "got it wrong" and sat down with them.
He was later accused of rolling his eyes and crossing his arms during the meeting, a characterisation he rejected. The Coalition jumped on claims Keogh had disrespected veterans and called for him to be sacked.
Everything must go
Lambie's speech cut through in a week where there was a lot going on. A smorgasbord of deals saw the prime minister get a trio of inconvenient bills off his plate.
It was, as David Speers wrote this morning, a week of rare cooperation between the government and the opposition. After striking an agreement with the Greens on negative gearing, the capital gains tax, and environment laws it was now the Coalition's turn.
A bipartisan deal was struck on gambling advertising, which will result in the establishment of an opt-out register for online wagering ads. How that would work, well, that's anyone's guess right now.
The overhaul will be reviewed in three years' time, well after the next election. The debate was emotive across all sides of politics, especially for those who arrived in parliament at the same time as the late Labor MP Peta Murphy. She chaired the inquiry that led to the landmark report that called for online advertising and inducements to be banned completely.
Her husband, Rod Glover, this week wrote on LinkedIn to say the reforms were limited due to the "considerable power of the gambling industry, major sporting codes and media organisations".
Loading...
Albanese was also willing to give the Coalition another win on the so-called "widow's tax" — an issue the opposition plucked from David Pocock to champion.
The government says it was always going to patch up the unintended loophole left behind in its changes to negative gearing that removed the grandfathering arrangements in the case of inheritance or where a property was transferred by death or divorce. But the fix was in the tranche of legislation it pushed out to later in the year.
Given nobody objected to its draft changes, Albanese said he it took him a "millisecond" to give the green light to put it on the fast-track after Angus Taylor issued an ultimatum to do so or else the Coalition would hold up the overhaul to the National Disability Insurance Scheme.
Hanson continues to loom large
As much as this week was about the differentiation between what the major parties can achieve by cooperating and what One Nation is promising in protest, Pauline Hanson still hung heavy over debate.
Her declaration that cash-strapped Australians struggling with the cost of living should be able to access their superannuation early was easy morning television fodder — and a fight for Jim Chalmers to jump on.
The treasurer's office is in the much-coveted blue carpeted ministerial wing. So, it's always curious to find him at the House of Representatives doors where reporters with cameras lay in wait to ask backbench MPs questions about the news of the day as they roll into work.
He declared the next election would be a referendum on super. It's a fight he's been wanting to have. And it's a fight that got quickly personal when Labor sent text messages to supporters taking aim at Hanson and Liberal senator Andrew Bragg (who also isn't shy in his opinions on super), while asking for a donation to the party.
Hanson said the treasurer was "full of sh**" while Bragg called him an "absolute loser".
Meanwhile, a fight is also brewing over the tobacco excise. One Nation thinks it can wipe out the booming tobacco black market by cutting almost $30 off the cost of a packet of cigarettes — or slashing the excise by 75 per cent.
"The only way we can deal with the criminality, the mafia that also underwrites so many other drugs, is to bring back the price of legal cigarettes to the same realm as where illegal ones are," Barnaby Joyce told ABC's Radio National Breakfast.
To one-up One Nation, the Coalition is now reportedly considering an 80 per cent cut. The government on the other hand doesn't want to get in the "habit of engaging in price competition with organised crime" and sees this as an enforcement issue.
Loading
After the deals, comes the fight
With a meeting of the national cabinet on the agenda for next week, the prime minister will be gearing up for another round of tense negotiations.
From the GST carve-up to the NDIS and data centres, there is no shortage of fights the premiers could pick. Likewise, the prime minister will have a bone to pick with everyone but New South Wales over the national gun buyback scheme. Several state treasurers this week essentially told the ABC: "thanks, but no thanks".
But hey, at least he won't be talking about melons.

Russia strikes drone and missile plants in Kiev – MOD
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:05:05 UTC
16:05:05 KST

Russia strikes drone and missile plants in Kiev – MOD
Russian forces have carried out a large-scale strike against drone-making plants and other facilities in Kiev linked to the Ukrainian military, the Defense Ministry in Moscow has said.
The latest overnight raid involved ground-, air-, and sea-launched missiles as well as attack drones, the ministry said in a statement on Thursday morning.
An industrial facility belonging to weapons maker Fire Point was hit during the bombardment, the ministry said, adding that the plant had manufactured components for long- and medium-range drones and had also assembled and stored boosters for Flamingo cruise missiles.
Ukrainian leader Vladimir Zelensky actively promotes Fire Point and its products internationally, despite the firm being linked to his longtime business associate Timur Mindich, who fled Ukraine after becoming a key figure in a $100 million corruption scandal last year.
Another target was the Antonov aviation plant, where An-196 Liutyi long-range fixed-wing drones and other types of UAVs were designed and produced, the ministry said.
A large warehouse where UAVs were manufactured and stored, an aviation industry assembly plant, and an arms depot hosting vacuum bombs, anti-tank mines, and ammunition for Baba Yaga drones were also struck in the Ukrainian capital, the statement read.
In Kiev Region, Russian missiles and drones struck the Martusovka logistics center, used to store and distribute dual-use goods, including those intended for the production of UAVs and electronic warfare systems, it said.
According to the statement, a fuel and lubricants depot housing up to 8,000 cubic meters of fuel intended for the Ukrainian military was blown up in the town of Brovary, east of the capital.
The Ukrainian Emergencies Ministry said12 people were killed and more than 30 others wounded in the bombardment.
Ukraine has also carried another major drone raid against Russia overnight, with 726 UAVs destroyed by air defenses in 20 regions of the country, the Russian Defense Ministry said.
Moscow has condemned Ukrainian drone incursions, which target civilian infrastructure and residential areas inside Russia, as terrorist attacks aimed at compensating for Kiev’s setbacks on the battlefield, where Russian forces continue their steady advance.
According to Russian officials, the intensification of long-range missile and drone strikes against targets in Ukraine is a response to those actions.
Moscow maintains that it does not attack civilians, with its strikes being directed solely at military targets and infrastructure supporting Kiev’s forces.

Iranian foreign minister says Trump’s ‘Economic D-Day’ will bring US ‘further defeat’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-20
07:04:32 UTC
16:04:32 KST

Sahin Demir
20 August 2026•Update: 20 August 2026
Iran's Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said Thursday that US President Donald Trump’s newly announced “Economic D-Day” campaign against Tehran would bring Washington “further defeat” and deepen hostility among Iranians toward the US.
“The so-called ‘Economic D-Day’ is a diversion from America’s own crisis: unprecedented debt & surging interest costs,” Araghchi said on US social media company X.
“Doubling down on failed policies will only bring further defeat and enmity of Iranians,” he said, adding that what he called US “economic terrorism” threatens the global economy and sovereignty worldwide.
His remarks came after Trump announced Wednesday what he called “the most crushing economic operation ever taken against any country,” describing the campaign as “Economic Warfare and Isolation on an unprecedented scale.”
Trump said Washington would seek to cut off financial and commercial channels supporting Tehran and warned that countries allowing their financial institutions, businesses, airports, or government entities to provide Iran with an economic “lifeline” would face severe economic consequences.
Calling the campaign an “Economic D-Day,” Trump also urged US allies to join Washington in isolating Iran.
